Shuffle algebras, lattice paths and the commuting scheme Thanks: We thank Allen Knutson, Peter MacNamara, Andrei Negut, Gufang Zhao for discussions.
Alexandr Garbali, Paul Zinn-Justin

Abstract
. The commutative trigonometric shuffle algebra A {\mathrm{A}} is a space of symmetric rational functions satisfying certain wheel conditions [ FO97 , FHH+09 ] . We describe a ring isomorphism between A {\mathrm{A}} and the center of the Hecke algebra using a realization of the elements of A {\mathrm{A}} as partition functions of coloured lattice paths associated to the R R -matrix of 𝒰 t 1 / 2 ​ ( g ​ l ^ ∞ ) \mathcal{U}_{t^{1/2}}(\widehat{gl}_{\infty}) . As an application, we compute under certain conditions the Hilbert series of the commuting scheme and identify it with a particular element of the shuffle algebra A {\mathrm{A}} , thus providing a combinatorial formula for it as a “domain wall” type partition function of coloured lattice paths.



Dedicated to Jasper Stokman on the occasion of his 50th birthday

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Center of Hecke and shuffle products

Consider the
Hecke algebra

{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
(
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
), which we define for our purposes to be the algebra (over a field extension
{\mathbb{F}}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}(t)
to be specified below) with generators

T_{1},\ldots,T_{n-1}
and relations

T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\qquad T_{i}^{2}=(t-1)T_{i}+t\qquad T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\ (|i-j|\geq 2)

The standard basis
(T_{w})_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
is obtained by writing
T_{w}:=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k}}

if
w=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k}}
is a reduced word in the elementary transpositions
s_{i}
. Denote by
|w|=k
the length of
w
.

Combine the centers
{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
into a graded algebra

{\mathrm{Z}}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})

by defining a shuffle product

(1)

\displaystyle*:{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{k})\otimes{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\to{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{k+\ell})

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}}t^{-|w|}T_{w}xT_{w^{-1}}

where
{\mathcal{H}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{\ell}
is embedded inside
{\mathcal{H}}_{k+\ell}
in the obvious way, and
\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}
is the set of shortest representatives of
cosets in
\mathcal{S}_{k+\ell}/(\mathcal{S}_{k}\times\mathcal{S}_{\ell})
.

Let us also consider
\Lambda
, the algebra of
symmetric functions
with coefficients in
{\mathbb{F}}
. One can for example define it as a polynomial ring in countably many variables:
\Lambda:={\mathbb{F}}[p_{1},p_{2},\ldots]
where
\deg p_{r}=r
.

There is a graded algebra isomorphism
\Phi
between
{\mathrm{Z}}
and
\Lambda

[
WW15
]
, which will be reviewed in §
2
;
it is a deformation of the classical Frobenius map in the context of
the symmetric group.

In what follows we fix the base field to be

{\mathbb{F}}={\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)

Define
{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]_{0}
to be the space of Laurent polynomials with specific degree bounds:

(2)

{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]_{0}={\mathbb{F}}\text{-span of }\left\{x_{1}^{i_{1}}\ldots x_{n}^{i_{n}},\ i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\ \left|\sum_{j=1}^{r}i_{j}\right|\leq r(n-r),\ 0\leq r\leq n\right\}

(in particular, these are homogeneous polynomials of degree
0
)
and

{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]^{\mathcal{S}_{n}}_{0}
to be its subspace of symmetric Laurent polynomials.

Consider
1
1

1

{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
is not quite the shuffle algebra as normally defined, but is more convenient for us; the standard shuffle algebra, denoted by
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, will be given in §
3
.

{\mathrm{A}}_{n}=\left\{P\in{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]^{\mathcal{S}_{n}}_{0}:\ P(x,qx,tx,x_{4},\ldots,x_{n})=P(tqx,qx,tx,x_{4},\ldots,x_{n})=0\right\}

One can again introduce a shuffle product

\displaystyle*:{\mathrm{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathrm{A}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\to{\mathrm{A}}_{k+\ell}

\displaystyle P\otimes Q

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}}\left[P(x_{w(1)},\ldots,x_{w(k)})Q(x_{w(k+1)},\ldots,x_{w(k+\ell)})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
k+1\leq j\leq k+\ell\end{subarray}}\tilde{\omega}(x_{w(i)}/x_{w(j)})\right]

where
\tilde{\omega}(x)=\frac{(1-q^{-1}x)(1-qt^{-1}x)(1-tx)}{-x(1-x)}

making

{\mathrm{A}}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}{\mathrm{A}}_{n}

a graded algebra. There is another graded algebra isomorphism
\Upsilon
from
{\mathrm{A}}
to
\Lambda

[
FHH+09
]
, which will be reviewed in §
3.3
.

It is natural to ask if there is an interpretation of the isomorphism
\Upsilon^{-1}\circ\Phi
from
{\mathrm{Z}}
to
{\mathrm{A}}
.
Note however that
\Lambda
possesses graded algebra automorphisms, and in particular
we shall allow ourselves to work modulo the automorphisms that rescale the variables
p_{r}
.

1.2.
Lattice paths

Consider a
n\times n
square grid, and a set of
n
paths entering from the left external edges of the grid and exiting at the top.
We label incoming paths on the left
1,\ldots,n
from top to bottom.
We say that a set of paths
P
has connectivity
\text{conn}(P)=w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}

if the path labelled
i
(i.e., the
i^{\rm th}
incoming path on the left counted from the top)
exits at the
w^{-1}(i)^{\rm th}
location at the top (counted from the left).

Example 1
.

2431
:

P
we associate a (Boltzmann) weight defined as the product over each vertex of the square grid
of a factor that depends on the configuration around that vertex:

where the convention is that colours can be substituted as long as one preserves the ordering;
that is, if two paths are present at a vertex, red (resp. green) stands for the one with the lower (resp. greater) label.

To any element
c=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}c_{v}T_{v}\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
(
c_{v}\in{\mathbb{F}}
) of the center of the Hecke algebra we associate
the
partition function

(4)

f(c):=\alpha_{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lattice paths }P\\
\text{on the }n\times n\text{ grid}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{wt}(P)c_{\text{conn}(P)}

where
\alpha_{n}=(q/t)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(1-t)^{-n}
is a constant which we introduce for convenience.

We can now formulate our first theorem:

Theorem 1
.

There is a commuting square of graded algebra morphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{q^{-1}}\sigma_{t}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}

where
\sigma_{q^{-1}}\sigma_{t}^{-1}
is the automorphism that sends
p_{r}
to
(-1)^{r-1}\frac{\scriptstyle(1-q)^{r}(1-t^{r})}{\scriptstyle(1-q^{r})(1-t)^{r}}p_{r}
.

1.3.
Partition function with connectivity
12\dots n

We consider here the special case of the unit
1_{n}\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
which corresponds to the partition function with connectivity
12\dots n

f(1_{n})=\alpha_{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lattice paths }P\\
\text{on the }n\times n\text{ grid}\\
\text{conn}(P)=1_{n}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{wt}(P)

Example 2
.

Here are the identity paths at
n=3
:

Theorem 2
.

The partition function
f(1_{n})
, as an element of
{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]^{\mathcal{S}_{n}}_{0}
, is determined by
f(1_{0})=1
and the recurrence relation

(5)

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})f(1_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}f(1_{n-1})[\hat{x_{i}}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(1-q^{-1}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-qt^{-1}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-tx_{i}/x_{j})}{-x_{j}/x_{i}(1-x_{j}/x_{i})}

where
f(1_{n-1})[\hat{x_{i}}]\in{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]^{\mathcal{S}_{n}}_{0}
.

In §
2.4
we will show that the partition functions
f(c)
can be interpreted using the
R
-matrices of
\mathcal{U}_{t^{1/2}}(\widehat{gl}_{\infty})
. This means that one can study them using the quantum inverse scattering method (QISM)
[
KBI97
]
(see also
[
Lam18
]
), and indeed the partition function
f(1_{n})
was already considered in
[
BW18
]
. We note, however, that the recurrence relation (
5
) has an unusual form from the point of view of the QISM due to the division by
x_{1}+\dots+x_{j}
at every
j^{\rm th}
step of the recursive computation of
f(1_{n})
. It is not clear to us if one can prove (
5
) or find an alternative recursion relation for
f(1_{n})
using the QISM. Our proof of (
5
) is indirect; it follows as a corollary of our proof of Theorem
1
given in §
4
.

1.4.
The commuting scheme

We now give a geometric interpretation of the partition function
f(1_{n})
of the previous section in terms of the commuting scheme. (The geometric interpretation of other
f(c)
is left for future work.)

Given
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, define the
commuting scheme

\mathfrak{C}_{n}
to be the affine scheme in

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})^{2}
consisting of pairs of commuting
n\times n
matrices:

(6)

\mathfrak{C}_{n}:=\{(A,B)\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}}):\ [A,B]=0\}

\mathfrak{C}_{n}
is known to be irreducible, of dimension
n(n+1)

[
MT55
]
, smooth in codimension 1
[
Pop08
]
, and we have the conjecture, generally attributed to Artin and Hochster in 1982:

Conjecture 1
.

\mathfrak{C}_{n}
is Cohen–Macaulay.

which would imply that
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
is reduced and normal.
This conjecture will not be addressed in the present paper.

The group
GL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times GL_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
acts on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})^{2}
by

(f,g)\in GL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times GL_{n}({\mathbb{C}}):\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}\mapsto f\begin{pmatrix}gAg^{-1}\\
gBg^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

and leaves
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
invariant.
In particular, the maximal torus
T=({\mathbb{C}}^{\times})^{2+n}
induces a
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2+n}
-grading of the corresponding
coordinate rings; explicitly,

\deg A_{ij}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{i+2}-\varepsilon_{j+2}\qquad\deg B_{ij}=\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{i+2}-\varepsilon_{j+2}\qquad i,j=1,\ldots,n

where
\varepsilon_{i}
is the unit vector with nonzero coordinate
i
.

We consider the Hilbert series
\chi(\mathfrak{C}_{n})
of the commuting scheme with
the multigrading above (i.e., the
T
-character of its coordinate ring);
in fact, it is convenient to consider instead
the
K
-polynomial
(or Poincaré polynomial) of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
, defined by

K_{n}=\frac{\chi(\mathfrak{C}_{n})}{\chi(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})^{2})}

Explicitly, introducing formal variables
q_{1},q_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
corresponding to
({\mathbb{C}}^{\times})^{2+n}
, one has

\chi(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})^{2})=\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(1-q_{1}x_{i}x_{j}^{-1})(1-q_{2}x_{i}x_{j}^{-1})}

K_{n}
is known to be a Laurent polynomial in
{\mathbb{Z}}[q_{1}^{\pm},q_{2}^{\pm},x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]
.
Furthermore, because of the
GL_{2}({\mathbb{C}})\times GL_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
-invariance of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
,

K_{n}
is invariant under the action
of the corresponding Weyl group, i.e., it is a
symmetric
Laurent polynomial in the variables

q_{1},q_{2}
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
separately.

Finally, one can perform the substitution
q_{i}\mapsto 1-q_{i},x_{i}\mapsto 1-x_{i}
in
K_{n}
, expand in power series and keep only the
lowest degree terms in those variables.
We obtain this way a homogeneous polynomial of degree
\operatorname{codim}\mathfrak{C}_{n}=n(n-1)
in

{\mathbb{Z}}[q_{1},q_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
(with its ordinary grading), called the
multidegree
of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
and denoted by

D_{n}:=\operatorname{mdeg}\mathfrak{C}_{n}

Our first result on the commuting scheme is the following:

Theorem 3
.

Assuming Conjecture
1
,
the following formula holds for the
K
-polynomial of the commuting scheme:

(7)

K_{n}=(q_{1}q_{2})^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}f(1_{n})

with the identification
q=q_{1}^{-1}
,
t=(q_{1}q_{2})^{-1}
.

Performing the substitution of variables above, it is convenient to slightly rearrange the Boltzmann weights of §
1.2
to:

where colours of paths can be substituted freely.

We can state our second result:

Theorem 4
.

The following formula holds for the multidegree of the commuting scheme:

(10)

D_{n}:=(q_{1}+q_{2})^{-n}\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lattice paths }P\\
\text{on the }n\times n\text{ grid}\\
\text{with identity connectivity}\end{subarray}}}\mathrm{wt}_{H}(P)

Note that Conjecture
1
and Theorem
3
imply Theorem
4
, as can be easily checked. However Theorem
4
can be proven independently of Conjecture
1
.

1.5.
Plan of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
In §
2
, we provide details on the Hecke algebra construction of lattice paths partition functions,
and on the map
\Phi
from the center of Hecke to symmetric functions.
In §
3
, we give details on the shuffle algebra, the map
\Upsilon
and derive the recurrence relations which are required for proving Theorems
1
-
3
. In §
4
, we prove our Theorem
1
by an explicit comparison of
\Phi
and
\Upsilon\circ f
, and obtain Theorem
2
as a corollary of all that precedes.
In §
5
, we turn to the geometry of the commuting scheme
and prove Theorems
3
and
4
, ending with some comments.

2.
Hecke algebra and lattice paths

2.1.
The Hecke algebra and its center

As in §
1.1
, we consider the Hecke algebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
, an
{\mathbb{F}}
-algebra with generators

T_{1},\ldots,T_{n-1}
and relations

T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\qquad T_{i}^{2}=(t-1)T_{i}+t\qquad T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\ (|i-j|\geq 2)

In this paper we choose
{\mathbb{F}}
to be
{\mathbb{Q}}(q,t)
, though we shall not need the variable
q
until §
2.3
.
Note
{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\cong{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\cong{\mathbb{F}}
.

We have the simple lemma:

Lemma 1
.

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{w}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T_{s_{i}w}&|s_{i}w|>|w|\\
(t-1)T_{w}+tT_{s_{i}w}&|s_{i}w|<|w|\end{cases}

\displaystyle T_{w}T_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T_{ws_{i}}&|ws_{i}|>|w|\\
(t-1)T_{w}+tT_{ws_{i}}&|ws_{i}|<|w|\end{cases}

expressing the action of
T_{i}
on the standard basis
(T_{w})_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}
, where
T_{w}:=T_{i_{1}}\ldots T_{i_{k}}

if
w=s_{i_{1}}\ldots s_{i_{k}}
is a reduced word in the elementary transpositions
s_{i}
, and
|w|=k
.

We shall often need two particular elements of the center
{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
, namely
the complete symmetrizer

(11)

S_{n}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}T_{w}

and the complete antisymmetrizer

(12)

A_{n}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(-t)^{-|w|}T_{w}

Both are primitive idempotents up to normalization.

Next, we consider the shuffle product structure:

Proposition 1
.

The product
*
in (
1
) is a well-defined map, making
{\mathrm{Z}}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
a graded (associative) algebra.

The nontrival part of the proof can be formulated by repeatedly applying Lemma
1
; instead,
we shall illustrate it by using the standard diagrammatic way to depict the Hecke algebra, as acting on
n
strands:

The arrows keep track of the ordering of operators: moving forward w.r.t. the orientation of a line is reading an expression right to left. Sometimes we will omit arrows; then all lines are implicitly oriented downwards.
(The diagrammatic calculus that follows can be viewed as warming up for the considerably more difficult proof of our Theorem
1
, which will also be diagrammatic.)

Proof.

Associativity of the product is obvious: an
m
-fold product can be expressed as

(13)

x_{1}*\cdots*x_{m}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}t^{-|w|}T_{w}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{m})T_{w^{-1}}

where
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\lambda_{i}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
is the set
of shortest representatives in
\mathcal{S}_{n}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
,

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}=\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{m}}
.

The only nontrivial part is to show that
*
is well-defined as a map from
{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{k})\otimes{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell})
to
{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{k+\ell})
, i.e., we need to show that the right hand side of (
1
) is central, or equivalently that it commutes with all
T_{i}
s.

We recall that
\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}
is the set of shortest representatives
in
\mathcal{S}_{k+\ell}/(\mathcal{S}_{k}\times\mathcal{S}_{\ell})
; equivalently,
it is the set of (Grassmannian) permutations of size
k+\ell
with a unique possible descent at
(k,k+1)
.

Fixing
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, we distinguish four subsets of
\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}
according to the preimages of
i
and
i+1
:

\mathcal{S}_{\lessgtr,\lessgtr}=\{w\in\mathcal{S}^{k,\ell}:\ w^{-1}(i)\lessgtr k+1/2,\ w^{-1}(i+1)\lessgtr k+1/2\}

Note that
w\mapsto s_{i}w
induces a bijection between
\mathcal{S}_{<,>}
and
\mathcal{S}_{>,<}
.
We now discuss the corresponding terms in (
1
) separately:

•

If
w\in\mathcal{S}_{<,<}
, then
T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}
commutes with
T_{i}

provided
a\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{k})
, as shown diagrammatically:

T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}T_{i}=\hbox to154.84pt{\vbox to94.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.95161pt\lower-35.94344pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=T_{i}T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}

•

Similarly, if
w\in\mathcal{S}_{>,>}
, then
T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}
commutes with
T_{i}

provided
b\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\ell})
.

•

Finally, we group together the two terms corresponding to
w\in\mathcal{S}_{<,>}
and
s_{i}w\in\mathcal{S}_{>,<}
to obtain:

(T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}+t^{-1}T_{s_{i}w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}s_{i}})T_{i}\\

=T_{w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}s_{i}}+T_{s_{i}w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}}+(1-t^{-1})T_{s_{i}w}(a\otimes b)T_{w^{-1}s_{i}}

Clearly, the same result is obtained by multiplying by
T_{i}
on the left.

By multiplying by
t^{-|w|}
and summing over
w
, we obtain the desired commutation property for the right hand side of (
1
).
∎

We define an algebra morphism
\Psi
from
\Lambda={\mathbb{F}}[p_{1},p_{2},\ldots]
to
{\mathrm{Z}}
as follows.
Define Jucys–Murphy elements in
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
(see e.g.
[
Ram97
]
) to be

J_{j,n}=\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}t^{i-j+1}T_{i}T_{i+1}\ldots T_{j-1}T_{j-2}\ldots T_{i},\qquad j=2,\ldots,n

They form a commutative subalgebra of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
, and it is well-known that symmetric polynomials in the
J_{j,n}
are central. Then
\Psi
sends
p_{n}
to the element

\Psi(p_{n})=[n]_{t^{-1}}\prod_{j=2}^{n}J_{j,n}

where
[n]_{t^{-1}}=1+\cdots+t^{-(n-1)}
.

Proposition 2

(
[
WW15
]
)
.

\Psi
is a graded algebra isomorphism from
\Lambda
to
{\mathrm{Z}}
.

Its inverse is the
\Phi
of §
1.1
.
As a corollary,
{\mathrm{Z}}
is commutative.

Denote by

(14)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{n}

\displaystyle=\Phi(1_{n})

(15)

\displaystyle\tilde{e}_{n}

\displaystyle=\Phi(t^{-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}T_{w_{0}}^{2})

(16)

\displaystyle h_{n}

\displaystyle=\Phi(S_{n})

(17)

\displaystyle e_{n}

\displaystyle=\Phi(A_{n})

where
1_{n}
is the identity of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
, and
w_{0}
the longest element of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. We can identify these symmetric functions.
Given
u\in{\mathbb{F}}
, define
\sigma_{u}
to be the automorphism of
\Lambda

(18)

\sigma_{u}:\ p_{r}\mapsto\frac{(1-u)^{r}}{1-u^{r}}\,p_{r}

Proposition 3

(
[
Las06
]
)
.

h_{n}
(resp.
e_{n})
is the complete (resp. elementary) symmetric function of degree
n
.

Furthermore,
\tilde{h}_{n}=\sigma_{t}(h_{n})
and
\tilde{e}_{n}=\sigma_{t}(e_{n})
.

We also denote by
\sigma_{\infty}:\ p_{r}\mapsto(-1)^{r-1}p_{r}
.
Note
\sigma_{u^{-1}}=\sigma_{\infty}\sigma_{u}
. It is well-known that
\sigma_{\infty}
exchanges
h_{n}
and
e_{n}
(and similarly for
\tilde{h}_{n}
and
\tilde{e}_{n}
).

2.2.
Partition functions from the Hecke algebra

We now work inside the algebra
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
. There are two obvious embeddings of
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
into
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
,
given by
T_{i}\mapsto T_{i}
and
T_{i}\mapsto T_{i+n}
respectively, and we denote the corresponding subalgebras by

{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{(1)}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{(2)}
.
Similarly, denote by
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}
the two subgroups of
\mathcal{S}_{2n}
(both isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{n}
) acting nontrivially on
\{1,\ldots,n\}
(resp.
\{n+1,\ldots,2n\}
) only.

Also define the
R
-matrix

(19)

\check{R}_{i}(u)=1-t+(1-u)T_{i}\qquad i=1,\ldots,2n-1

as an element of
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}[u^{\pm}]
.

Extending the diagrammatic calculus introduced in the proof of Proposition
1
, we depict
R
-matrices as (flat) crossings of two lines carrying variables (so-called spectral parameters), the parameter of the
R
-matrix being the ratio of spectral parameters:

The central object of study of this section is the “formal partition function”, which is the following element of
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm},y_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,y_{n}^{\pm}]
:

(22)

\displaystyle Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{n}(x_{n}/y_{1})\check{R}_{n-1}(x_{n-1}/y_{1})\check{R}_{n+1}(x_{n}/y_{2})\ldots\check{R}_{n}(x_{1}/y_{n})

\displaystyle=\hbox to117.02pt{\vbox to114.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-13.37718pt\lower 14.02638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

We also write
Z=Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
when there is no need to emphasize the dependence on the variables.

Z
has an expansion of the form

(23)

Z=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}Z_{w}T_{w}

where each
Z_{w}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[t^{\pm},x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm},y_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,y_{n}^{\pm}]
.

2.3.
Exchange relation and symmetry

Consider the operator
\tau_{i}
(resp.
\tau^{\prime}_{j}
) that permutes variables
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}

(resp.
y_{j}
and
y_{j+1}
),
i,j=1,\ldots,n-1
.

We have the following fundamental lemma:

Lemma 2
.

Z
satisfies the exchange relations:

(24)

\displaystyle(\tau_{i}Z)\check{R}_{n+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})

\displaystyle=\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z\qquad i=1,\ldots,n-1

\scriptstyle y_{1}
\scriptstyle y_{2}
\scriptstyle\cdots
\scriptstyle y_{n}
\scriptstyle x_{1}
\scriptstyle x_{n}
\scriptstyle\vdots
\scriptstyle\vdots
\scriptstyle x_{i+1}
\scriptstyle x_{i}

\displaystyle=\hbox to148.23pt{\vbox to114.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.42638pt\lower 14.02638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}

(25)

\displaystyle\check{R}_{n+j}(y_{j}/y_{j+1})\tau^{\prime}_{j}Z

\displaystyle=Z\check{R}_{j}(y_{j}/y_{j+1})\qquad j=1,\ldots,n-1

\scriptstyle y_{1}\hbox to0.0pt{\ $\scriptstyle\cdots$\hss}

Proof.

Both equalities result from repeated application of the Yang–Baxter equation (
20
).
∎

We now consider the specialization
y_{i}=qx_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
We first note

Lemma 3
.

Z_{w}|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}\in{\mathbb{F}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]_{0}
.

Proof.

Recall that each of the
n^{2}
factors of
Z
corresponds to some vertex
(i,j)
on the diagram of (
22
), and is a polynomial of degree
1
in the parameter
x_{i}/y_{j}
, so after specialization of the Lemma, in
x_{i}/(qx_{j})
. Now fix
r\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
and consider the degree
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}
in the first
r
variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}
.
Among the factors of
Z
, the ones corresponding in the diagram to the top-left
r\times r
square have degree zero, and similarly for the bottom
(n-r)\times(n-r)
square. The ones for the top-right
r\times(n-r)
square can have monomials of degree
0
or
1
, whereas for the bottom-left
(n-r)\times r
square they can be of degree
0
or
-1
. Summing these degrees, we find the desired inequality
|i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}|\leq r(n-r)
.
∎

Of course the same proof would work with any
r
-subset of variables,
suggesting that we should study the behaviour of
Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})
under the interchange of the variables
x_{i}
. We have the following condition:

Proposition 4
.

Let
\chi
be a linear form on
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
.

\chi(Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n}))
is a symmetric Laurent polynomial in the
x_{i}
s provided
\chi
satisfies

(26)

\chi(ab)=\chi(ba)\qquad\forall a\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2n},\ b\in{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{(1)}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{(2)}

Proof.

We assume
n\geq 2
, otherwise the Proposition is trivial.
By definition exchanging
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
in
\chi(Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n}))
amounts to considering

\chi(\tau_{i}\tau^{\prime}_{i}Z)

and then substituting
y_{i}=qx_{i}
. One has

\chi(\tau_{i}\tau^{\prime}_{i}Z)=\chi(\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})\check{R}_{n+i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})^{-1}Z\check{R}_{i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})\check{R}_{n+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})^{-1})

Assuming the property (
26
) of the Proposition, one can rewrite this

\chi(\tau_{i}\tau^{\prime}_{i}Z)=\chi(\check{R}_{i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z\check{R}_{n+i}(y_{i}/y_{i+1})^{-1}\check{R}_{n+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})^{-1})

Substituting
y_{i}=qx_{i}
and simplifying using unitarity equation (
21
), we obtain the desired symmetry of
\chi(Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n}))
.

∎

In what follows, we restrict ourselves to the following class of linear forms.
Denote by
\left<\bullet\right>
the linear form that extracts the coefficient of
1
in a linear combination of
T_{w}
, i.e.,
\left<T_{w}\right>:=\delta_{w,1}
. One checks that

\left<ab\right>=\left<ba\right>=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}t^{|w|}a_{w}b_{w^{-1}},\qquad a=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}a_{w}T_{w},\ b=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}b_{w}T_{w}

Given
c\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
, we then define

(27)

f(c):=\alpha_{n}\left<(c\otimes S_{n})Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})\right>

where
S_{n}
was defined in (
11
), and
\alpha_{n}=(q/t)^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}(1-t)^{-n}
is the same constant as in the introduction.
Clearly,
\chi=\left<(c\otimes S_{n})\bullet\right>
fulfills the condition of Proposition
4
.

Example 4
.

Three important examples are:

•

the identity:
f(1_{n})
is the object of §
1.3
and §
5
.

•

the complete antisymmetrizer:
f(A_{n})
will be computed in §
4
.

•

the complete symmetrizer:
f(S_{n})
is also interesting, since the symmetrization makes the model “color-blind”, and thus equal to the famous Izergin determinant
[
Ize87
]
for the partition function of the six-vertex model with domain wall boundary conditions, as can be shown by the usual symmetry and recurrence arguments
[
Kor82
]
. We leave the proof to the interested reader.

We finally introduce a lattice path representation of
f(c)
, reconnecting
it to the content of §
1.2
.

2.4.
Lattice path representation

Consider the following representation of
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
.
As a vector space,
\mathcal{M}=({\mathbb{F}}^{n+1})^{\otimes 2n}
.
Letting
(e_{i})_{i=1,\ldots,n+1}
be the standard basis of
{\mathbb{F}}^{n+1}
, define the action of
{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
by

T_{i}(e_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{k_{2n}})=\sum_{k^{\prime}_{i},k^{\prime}_{i+1}=1}^{n+1}\begin{bmatrix}&k_{i}&\\
k^{\prime}_{i}&&k_{i+1}\\
&k^{\prime}_{i+1}&\end{bmatrix}e_{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{k_{i-1}}\otimes e_{k^{\prime}_{i}}\otimes e_{k^{\prime}_{i+1}}\otimes e_{k_{i+2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{k_{2n}}

where

(28)

\begin{bmatrix}&i&\\
j&&k\\
&\ell&\end{bmatrix}=\begin{cases}t-1&i=j>k=\ell\\
1&j=k>i=\ell\\
t&j=k\leq i=\ell\\
0&\text{else}\end{cases}

Given
v\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}
, define

\left|v\right\rangle=e_{\omega(v(1))}\otimes\cdots\otimes e_{\omega(v(2n))}

where
\omega(i)=i
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\omega(i)=n+1
for
n+1\leq i\leq 2n
.
Note that
\left|v\right\rangle
only depends on the class of
v
inside
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
; we define this way
a basis indexed by
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
of the submodule of
\mathcal{M}
of interest to us.

Similarly, define dual basis elements

\left\langle v\right|=e^{*}_{\omega(v(1))}\otimes\cdots\otimes e^{*}_{\omega(v(2n))}

in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

We have the easy lemma

Lemma 4
.

For all
w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}
,

(a)

\left\langle 1\right|T_{w}=t^{|w|}\left\langle w\right|
.

(b)

T_{w}\left|1\right\rangle=t^{|w_{2}|}\left|w^{-1}\right\rangle
where
w_{2}
is defined by
w^{-1}=w_{1}w_{2}
,

w_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}
and
w_{2}
a minimal representative in
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
.

Proof.

(a) Write
w=w_{1}w_{2}
where
w_{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}
and
w_{2}
minimal representative in
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
.
One has directly
\left\langle 1\right|T_{w_{1}}=t^{|w_{1}|}\left\langle 1\right|
. Then use a reduced decomposition of
w_{2}
and note that
in (
28
), one only ever uses the case
j<\ell
which leads to
\left\langle 1\right|T_{w_{2}}=t^{|w_{2}|}\left\langle w_{2}\right|
inductively. At both stages, the only nonzero contributions come from the third line of (
28
).
Case (b) is similar except the first (resp. second) stage involves the second (resp. third) line of (
28
).

∎

Define, noting that
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{n}
naturally sits inside
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
,

(29)

F_{v}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=\left\langle 1\right|Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\left|v\right\rangle\qquad v\in\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{n}

This is a slightly more general definition than we need for the interpretation of the right hand side of (
4
); indeed, we have:

Proposition 5
.

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lattice paths }P\\
\text{on the }n\times n\text{ grid}\\
\text{conn}(P)=v\end{subarray}}\mathrm{wt}(P)=F_{v}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})

Proof.

Consider the diagrammatic representation (
22
) of
Z
and apply the representation described at the start of this section; this amounts to assigning to each edge a label in
\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
.

F_{v}
corresponds, in accordance with (
29
), to further imposing that labels of external edges be fixed:
1,\ldots,n
on the left side,
v(1),\ldots,v(n)
on the top side, and
n+1
on the right and bottom sides. Now identify labels
1,\ldots,n
with the corresponding lattice paths, and
n+1
with “empty”. Then this produces precisely a lattice path with connectivity
P
. Finally, what needs to be checked is that the weight assigned to a lattice path coincides with the entry of
Z
. This can be done locally at the level of each individual crossing (
R
-matrix); and indeed, combining (
28
) with (
19
) and setting
y_{i}=qx_{i}
leads to the weights in (
3
).
∎

Finally, we can show that the definition (
27
) given above is equivalent to the one (
4
) in the introduction:

Lemma 5
.

If
c=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}c_{v}T_{v}\in{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
, then
c_{v}=c_{v^{-1}}
for all
v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
and

f(c)=\alpha_{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}c_{v}F_{v}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})

Proof.

First we observe that

(30)

\left<x(1\otimes S_{n})\right>=\left\langle 1\right|x\left|1\right\rangle\qquad\forall x\in{\mathcal{H}}_{2n}

by testing this on
x=T_{w}
,
w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}
, and noting that
both sides of the equality are
0
unless
w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}^{(2)}
,
in which case they are equal to
t^{|w|}
(using the definition (
11
) for the left hand side and Lemma
4
for the right hand side).
We now start from the definition (
27
) of
f(c)
and apply the equality above, as well as Lemma
4
(b):

f(c)=\alpha_{n}\left\langle 1\right|Zc\left|1\right\rangle=\alpha_{n}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}c_{v^{-1}}F_{v}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})

This is almost (
4
), except a small simplification was made:
any central element satisfies
c_{v}=c_{v^{-1}}

(short proof: it is obviously true for the generators
S_{n}
of
{\mathrm{Z}}
, and
*
preserves this property).
∎

We conclude this section by reformulating more explicitly the exchange relations of Lemma
2
in terms of the
F_{v}
. The next proposition will not be used in what follows, but is included to reconnect to the existing literature
[
BW18
]
.

Proposition 6
.

For
v\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(1)}
and
i,j=1,\ldots,n-1
,

\displaystyle(1-tx_{i+1}/x_{i})\tau_{i}F_{v}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(1-t)F_{v}+(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{s_{i}v}&s_{i}v>v\\
(1-t)(x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{v}+t(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{s_{i}v}&s_{i}v<v\end{cases}

\displaystyle(1-ty_{j}/y_{j+1})\tau^{\prime}_{j}F_{v}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(1-t)F_{v}+(1-y_{j}/y_{j+1})F_{vs_{j}}&vs_{j}>v\\
(1-t)(y_{j}/y_{j+1})F_{v}+t(1-y_{j}/y_{j+1})F_{vs_{j}}&vs_{j}<v\end{cases}

Proof.

First, note that (
23
), (
29
), and Lemma
4
(a) imply

(31)

F_{v}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}v}t^{|w|}Z_{w}

where the summation is over the coset of
v
in
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{n}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{2n}
.

We then start from the identity (
24
) of Lemma
2
.

Using (
23
) and Lemma
1
,
we obtain for the right hand side:

\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}Z_{w}\begin{cases}(1-t)T_{w}+(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})T_{s_{i}w}&s_{i}w>w\\
(1-t)(x_{i+1}/x_{i})T_{w}+t(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})T_{s_{i}w}&s_{i}w<w\end{cases}

We now take the bra-ket
\left\langle 1\right|\cdot\left|v\right\rangle
and note that only
w
such that either
w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}v
or
w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}s_{i}v
contribute.
We get for
s_{i}v>v

\displaystyle\left\langle 1\right|\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z\left|v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=(1-t)\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}v}t^{|w|}Z_{w}+t(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}s_{i}v}t^{|s_{i}w|}Z_{w}

\displaystyle=(1-t)F_{v}+(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{s_{i}v}

and for
s_{i}v<v

\displaystyle\left\langle 1\right|\check{R}_{i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})Z\left|v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=(1-t)(x_{i+1}/x_{i})\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}v}t^{|w|}Z_{w}+(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}s_{i}v}t^{|s_{i}w|}Z_{w}

\displaystyle=(1-t)(x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{v}+t(1-x_{i+1}/x_{i})F_{s_{i}v}

The bra-ket
\left\langle 1\right|\cdot\left|v\right\rangle
of the left hand side is simple to evaluate: we note that
\check{R}_{n+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})\left|v\right\rangle=(1-tx_{i+1}/x_{i})\left|v\right\rangle
because the
i^{\rm th}
and
(i+1)^{\rm th}
factors of
\left|v\right\rangle
are
e_{n+1}\otimes e_{n+1}
;
therefore

\left\langle 1\right|(\tau_{i}Z)\check{R}_{n+i}(x_{i+1}/x_{i})\left|v\right\rangle=(1-tx_{i+1}/x_{i})\tau_{i}F_{v}

This proves the first identity of the Proposition.

The second identity is actually simpler to prove (there isn’t a full symmetry between rows and columns).
Start analogously from (
25
) of Lemma
2
.
Now apply the bra-ket
\left\langle 1\right|\cdot\left|v\right\rangle
directly. One has
\left\langle 1\right|\check{R}_{n+j}(y_{j}/y_{j+1})=(1-ty_{j}/y_{j+1})\left\langle 1\right|

by the same argument as above;
but one also has from (
28
)

\check{R}_{j}(y_{j}/y_{j+1})\left|v\right\rangle=\begin{cases}(1-t)\left|v\right\rangle+(1-y_{j}/y_{j+1})\left|vs_{j}\right\rangle&vs_{j}>v\\
(y_{j}/y_{j+1})(1-t)\left|v\right\rangle+t(1-y_{j}/y_{j+1})\left|vs_{j}\right\rangle&vs_{j}<v\end{cases}

which leads to the desired identity.
∎

These relations can be used to determine the
F_{v}
inductively. More precisely, consider first the computation of
F_{w_{0}}
where
w_{0}
is the longest element of
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(1)}
. There is a unique lattice path with such connectivity, of the form

from which we conclude

(32)

F_{w_{0}}=(1-t)^{n}t^{\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
i+j<n+1\end{subarray}}(1-x_{i}/y_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
i+j>n+1\end{subarray}}(1-tx_{i}/y_{j})

One can then apply
either
the second relation or the fourth relation of Proposition
6
to define
the
F_{w}
inductively.

3.
The shuffle algebra

This section is devoted to the trigonometric Feigin–Odesskii shuffle algebra
[
FO97
]
. It was studied notably in
[
FHH+09
,
FT11
,
SV13
,
Neg14
]
. We will review some known results and derive several formulas which are required for proving Theorems
1
-
3
. We will follow the conventions of
[
FHH+09
]
and define the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}
which is slightly different from the algebra
{\mathrm{A}}
in §
1.1
. The precise connection between
\mathcal{A}
and
{\mathrm{A}}
is given in Remark
1
. For the treatment of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}
it is convenient to use three parameters
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
, expressed via
q
and
t
as

(33)

\displaystyle q_{1}=q^{-1}\qquad q_{2}=qt^{-1}\qquad q_{3}=(q_{1}q_{2})^{-1}=t

Let us outline the goals of this section:

•

Discuss the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
, its commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{A}
and the isomorphism
\Upsilon
of Theorem
1

•

Give a characterization of the distinguished basis elements
\epsilon_{\lambda}
of the shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}
via specializations which will be used in §
4
to prove Theorem
1

•

Introduce a special shuffle element
\kappa_{n}
which will be used in §
4
to prove Theorem
2
and in §
5.1
to prove Theorem
3

3.1.
The shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}

The shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is a vector space whose elements are symmetric rational functions of the form

(34)

\displaystyle P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}\qquad p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{F}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]^{\mathcal{S}_{n}}

where
p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
satisfies the
wheel condition

(35)

\displaystyle p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=0\quad\text{if}\quad(x_{i},x_{j},x_{k})=(x,q_{1}x,q_{1}q_{2}x)\quad\text{or}\quad(x_{i},x_{j},x_{k})=(x,q_{2}x,q_{1}q_{2}x)

The number of arguments
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
gives a grading

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{+}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{+}

We set
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{+}=\mathbb{F}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{+}=\mathbb{F}[x^{\pm 1}]
.
The shuffle algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is closed under the
shuffle product

*
, defined for two elements
F\in\mathcal{A}_{k}^{+}
and
G\in\mathcal{A}_{l}^{+}
by

(36)

\displaystyle(F*G)(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+l})=\frac{1}{k!l!}\text{Sym}\left[F(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})G(x_{k+1},\dots,x_{k+l})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
1\leq j\leq l\end{subarray}}\omega(x_{k+j},x_{i})\right]

where
Sym
is the symmetrization over all arguments
x_{i}

(37)

\displaystyle\text{Sym}\left[f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\right]=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}f(x_{w(1)},\dots,x_{w(n)})

and
\omega(x,y)
is defined as follows

(38)

\displaystyle\omega(x,y)=\frac{(x-q_{1}y)(x-q_{2}y)(x-q_{3}y)}{(x-y)^{3}}

It was shown in
[
Neg14
]
that the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{+}
is generated by the elements
x^{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}^{+}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

3.2.
The commutative subalgebra
\mathcal{A}

Consider a subalgebra
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{A}^{+}
of the elements
P\in\mathcal{A}_{n}^{+}
for which the two limits

(39)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0}P(\xi x_{1},\dots,\xi x_{r},x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n}),

(40)

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\rightarrow\infty}P(\xi x_{1},\dots,\xi x_{r},x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})

exist and coincide for all fixed
r=1,\dots,n
. This subalgebra splits into components of fixed degree in the same way as
\mathcal{A}^{+}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathcal{A}_{n}

Remark 1
.

The shuffle algebras
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
are isomorphic. For
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
Q(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
, s.t.
Q\mapsto P
, we have

(41)

\displaystyle P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{1}{V_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}Q(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

where
V_{n}=V_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is defined by

(42)

V_{n}:=\prod_{1\leq i\neq j\leq n}(1-x_{i}/x_{j})

Proof.

The product
V_{n}^{-1}Q(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is of the form (
34
) and the wheel conditions of
\mathcal{A}_{n}^{+}
and
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
coincide. The fact that the limiting conditions (
39
) and (
40
) of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
are equivalent to the degree constraints (
2
) of
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
was shown in
[
FHH+09
]
.
∎

Another important result of
[
FHH+09
]
is the following proposition.

Proposition 7
.

The algebra
(\mathcal{A},*)
is commutative and the dimension of the graded subspace
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is equal to the number of partitions of
n
.

There are several distinguished bases in the space
\mathcal{A}
. These bases are built using the shuffle elements

(43)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q_{k})=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{k}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{k}^{-1}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}\qquad k=1,2,3

In the following we will abbreviate them as
\epsilon_{n}(q_{k})=\epsilon_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q_{k})
.
Due to commutativity the products
\epsilon_{i}(q_{k})\,*\,\epsilon_{j}(q_{k})
can be ordered such that
i>j
. Thus for a partition
\lambda
the elements

(44)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(q_{k})=\epsilon_{\lambda_{1}}(q_{k})*\epsilon_{\lambda_{2}}(q_{k})*\dots*\epsilon_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}(q_{k})

give three bases, one for each
k=1,2,3
.

3.3.
Macdonald operators and the isomorphism
\Upsilon

Define the Heisenberg algebra
\mathfrak{h}
with generators
a_{\pm n}
,
n>0
, satisfying the relations

(45)

\displaystyle[a_{m},a_{n}]=\delta_{m,-n}m\frac{1-q^{|m|}}{1-t^{|m|}}

We have two commutative subalgebras generated by
\{a_{\pm n}\}_{n>0}
and containing the elements
a_{\pm\lambda}=a_{\pm\lambda_{1}}\dots a_{\pm\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}
.
Define the Fock space
\mathcal{F}
by introducing the vacuum vector
\left|\emptyset\right\rangle
which is annihilated by the positive modes
a_{n}\left|\emptyset\right\rangle=0
, for
n>0
. The action of the negative modes creates a basis

(46)

\displaystyle\left|a_{\lambda}\right\rangle=a_{-\lambda}\left|\emptyset\right\rangle

for all partitions
\lambda
.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots)
be the alphabet for the ring of symmetric functions
\Lambda
with the base field
\mathbb{F}
. Recall the power sum basis of
\Lambda

(47)

\displaystyle p_{\lambda}=p_{\lambda_{1}}\dots p_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}\qquad p_{n}=x_{1}^{n}+x_{2}^{n}+\dots

The Fock space
\mathcal{F}
is isomorphic to
\Lambda
where the isomorphism is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\left|a_{\lambda}\right\rangle\mapsto p_{\lambda}

In
\Lambda
a symmetric function is considered in the power sum basis and the above isomorphism replaces it with a vector in the Fock space. A distinguished basis in
\Lambda
is given by the Macdonald symmetric functions
P_{\lambda}
(see
[
Mac98
]
). We view it as a basis in the Fock space denoted by
\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle
.

The Macdonald operators are defined as the operators which act diagonally on
P_{\lambda}
. The
first
Macdonald operator
E

[
Mac98
, Ch. VI]
acts as follows

(48)

\displaystyle E\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle=\sum_{i\geq 1}(q^{\lambda_{i}}-1)t^{-i}\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle

This operator is realized on the Fock space with the help of the vertex operator
[
Shi06
]

(49)

\displaystyle\eta(z):=\exp\left(\sum_{n>0}\frac{1-t^{-n}}{n}a_{-n}z^{n}\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{n>0}\frac{1-t^{n}}{n}a_{n}z^{-n}\right)

The Fourier modes of this operator
\eta_{n}
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
) are given by

\displaystyle\eta(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\eta_{n}z^{-n}

and have a well defined action on
\mathcal{F}
. The operator
E
is given by the zero mode
[
Shi06
]

(50)

\displaystyle\eta_{0}=(t-1)E+1

We can write the eigenvalue equation as follows

\displaystyle\frac{t^{-1}}{1-t^{-1}}\eta_{0}\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle=u_{\lambda}(e_{1})\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle

where

u_{\lambda}(x_{i})=q^{\lambda_{i}}t^{-i}

and
e_{1}
is the first elementary symmetric function in the infinite alphabet
e_{1}=x_{1}+x_{2}+\dots
. One can build higher order operators in a systematic way using the algebra
\mathcal{A}
and the operators
\eta
.

The product of Heisenberg operators is
normally ordered
if all positive modes are on the right and negative modes on the left. The product
\eta(z)\eta(w)
can be ordered at the expense of a rational function

(51)

\displaystyle\eta(z)\eta(w)=\frac{(1-w/z)(1-qt^{-1}w/z)}{(1-qw/z)(1-t^{-1}w/z)}:\eta(z)\eta(w):

where the operators inside
::
are normally ordered.
For
f\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
define the mapping
\mathcal{O}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\text{End}_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathcal{F})
by

(52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(f)=\frac{1}{(t-1)^{n}n!}\oint\frac{\text{d}z_{1}\cdots\text{d}z_{n}}{z_{1}\cdots z_{n}}\frac{f(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})}{\epsilon_{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};q_{1})\epsilon_{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};q_{3})}:\eta(z_{1})\dots\eta(z_{n}):

where the integration contours are given by
|z_{1}|=\dots=|z_{n}|=1
and
|t|,|q|<1
. It was shown in
[
FHH+09
]
that
\mathcal{O}
is compatible with the shuffle product, i.e.

(53)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(f*g)=\mathcal{O}(f)\mathcal{O}(g)

Since
\mathcal{A}
is commutative this implies the commutativity of the operators

[\mathcal{O}(f),\mathcal{O}(g)]=0
for any two elements
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
. The map
\mathcal{O}
was used in
[
FHH+09
]
to define an isomorphism
\Upsilon^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\Lambda
:

Proposition 8
.

Given
f\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, there exists an
S\in\Lambda_{n}
such that for any partition
\lambda
,

(54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(f)\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{\lambda}(S)\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle

and
\Upsilon^{\prime}:f\mapsto S
thus defined is a ring isomorphism from
\mathcal{A}
to
\Lambda
.

By inserting a
V_{n}^{-1}
in (
54
), cf Remark
1
, we obtain the isomorphism
\Upsilon
from
{\mathrm{A}}
to
\Lambda
that is stated in the introduction and used in Theorem
1
.
The symmetric functions in
\Lambda
which correspond to the shuffle elements
\epsilon_{n}(q_{k})
,
k=1,2,3
are all given by a plethystic substitution applied to the elementary symmetric functions
e_{n}
. Recalling (
18
) we set

(55)

\displaystyle e_{n}^{(1)}=e_{n}\qquad e_{n}^{(2)}=\sigma_{q^{-1}}\sigma^{-1}_{qt^{-1}}(e_{n})\qquad e_{n}^{(3)}=\sigma_{q^{-1}}\sigma_{t}^{-1}(e_{n})\qquad

Note that the plethystic substitution in
e_{n}^{(3)}
appears in the statement of Theorem
1
. This element will play a special role. We have
[
FHH+09
]

(56)

\Upsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{k}))=e^{(k)}_{n}\qquad k=1,2,3

or equivalently

(57)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{k}))\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=u_{\lambda}(e^{(k)}_{n})\left|P_{\lambda}\right\rangle\qquad k=1,2,3

The eigenvalue equation (
57
) for
k=1
shows that the operator
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{1}))
coincides with the
n^{\rm th}
Macdonald operator
[
Mac98
]
. Thus one shows (
57
) by matching the Macdonald difference operators
E_{n}
with
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{1}))
. Based on this result one can show the other two eigenvalue equations for
k=2,3
using the Wronski relations

(58)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}(q_{k}^{j}-q_{l}^{n-j})\frac{(1-q_{l})^{j}}{(1-q_{k})^{j}}\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})*\epsilon_{j}(q_{l})=0\qquad k,l=1,2,3

In
[
FHH+09
]
the authors proved these Wronski relations. Applying
\mathcal{O}
to (
58
) with
l=1,k=2,3
gives a relation between the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{1}))
and
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon_{n}(q_{k}))
which determines the eigenvalue in (
57
) for
k=2,3
.

We finish this section by giving a lemma which will be used at a later stage.

Lemma 6
.

The following identities hold

(59)

\displaystyle\left(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\right)\epsilon_{n}(q_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-q_{k}}\left(x_{1}*\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{k})-q_{k}\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{k})*x_{1}\right)\qquad k=1,2,3

Proof.

For simplicity we fix
k=1
. Let us denote the right hand side of (
59
) by
C_{n}
and rewrite it using the definition of the shuffle product (
36
)

\displaystyle C_{n}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{1})[\hat{x_{i}}]

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{j}-q_{1}x_{i})(x_{j}-q_{2}x_{i})(x_{j}-q_{3}x_{i})}{(x_{j}-x_{i})^{3}}-q_{1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{1}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{2}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{3}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{3}}\right)

where
\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{1})[\hat{x_{i}}]
depends on the
n-1
arguments
(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})
.
Noting that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{1})[\hat{x_{i}}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{j}-q_{1}x_{i})}{(x_{j}-x_{i})}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{n}(q_{1})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{j}-x_{i})}{(x_{j}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{i})}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n-1}(q_{1})[\hat{x_{i}}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{1}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-x_{j})}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{n}(q_{1})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{j})}{(x_{i}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{j})}

leads to

\displaystyle C_{n}=\epsilon_{n}(q_{1})\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{j}-q_{2}x_{i})(x_{j}-q_{3}x_{i})}{(x_{j}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{i})(x_{j}-x_{i})}-q_{1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{2}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{3}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{j})(x_{i}-x_{j})}\right)

The factor next to
\epsilon_{n}(q_{1})
does not have poles at
x_{i}=x_{j}
or
x_{i}=q_{1}^{-1}x_{j}
because of the symmetry in
x_{i}
and vanishing residues at
x_{i}=q_{1}^{-1}x_{j}
which can be easily verified. It means that this factor must be a symmetric polynomial of degree
1
. Thus it is proportional to the elementary symmetric polynomial
e_{1}
in
n
arguments

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-q_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{2}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{3}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{j})(x_{i}-x_{j})}-q_{1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{j}-q_{2}x_{i})(x_{j}-q_{3}x_{i})}{(x_{j}-q_{1}^{-1}x_{i})(x_{j}-x_{i})}\right)=C\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}

We need to show that the proportionality factor
C=1
. This can be verified by setting
x_{i}=0
for
i>1
in the above equation. This completes the proof of (
59
).
∎

3.4.
Shuffle elements of
\mathcal{A}
and specializations

In this section we look at the problem of determining the shuffle algebra elements of
\mathcal{A}
recursively via specializations of the arguments
x_{i}
. This problem was investigated in
[
Neg14
,
Neg16
]
using a coproduct map of
\mathcal{A}^{+}
. Our treatment of this problem is appropriate for the goal of matching shuffle elements with partition functions in §
4
.

Recall that the shuffle elements of
\mathcal{A}
have the form

(60)

\displaystyle P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}

where
p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is a symmetric polynomial. We start with a lemma which gives us information about the degree of
p(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
as in (
60
). Let
x
be an indeterminate and
c_{1},\dots,c_{r}\in\mathbb{F}
then
p(c_{1}x,\dots,c_{r}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{F}[x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n}]
is of the form

(61)

\displaystyle x^{r(r-1)}G(x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n};c_{1},\dots,c_{r})

and
G(x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n};c_{1},\dots,c_{r})
is a polynomial of degree at most
2r(n-r)
in
x
.

Proof.

Consider the specialization
x_{k}=c_{k}x

(k=1,\dots,r)
of (
60
)

P(c_{1}x,\dots,c_{r}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})\\
=\frac{p(c_{1}x,\dots,c_{r}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})}{x^{r(r-1)}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=r+1}^{n}(c_{i}x-x_{j})^{2}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(c_{i}-c_{j})^{2}\prod_{r+1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}

The existence of the limit (
39
) implies that, for
x\rightarrow 0
, the polynomial in the numerator
p(c_{1}x,\dots,c_{r}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})
contains an overall factor
x^{r(r-1)}
to compensate the same factor in the denominator. Thus we established the form (
61
) and

P(c_{1}x,\dots,c_{r}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})\\
=\frac{G(x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n};c_{1},\dots,c_{r})}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=r+1}^{n}(c_{i}x-x_{j})^{2}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(c_{i}-c_{j})^{2}\prod_{r+1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}

The existence of the limit (
40
) implies that, for
x\rightarrow\infty
, the degree in
x
of the polynomial
G(x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})
is controlled by
\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=r+1}^{n}(c_{i}x-x_{j})^{2}
, i.e. bounded by
2r(n-r)
.
∎

In the following we will only be interested in the situations when
c_{i}=q_{k}^{i-1}
.
The next statement gives a recipe for a recursive computation of shuffle elements of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proposition 9
.

Let
r\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
be an element of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
as in (
60
). Fix
k\in\{1,2,3\}
, then
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is determined by the following specializations:

(1.r)

\lim_{x_{i}\to 0}\cdots\lim_{x_{1}\to 0}P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n})
,
i=1,\ldots,r-1
.

(2.r)

P(x,q_{k}x,\ldots,q_{k}^{r-1}x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})
.

Proof.

We fix
k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}
are all distinct.
The proof is by induction on
r
. The case
r=1
is trivial.
Now assume the property true at
r\geq 1
, i.e. knowing
(1.r)
and
(2.r)
allows us to compute
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
. Let us show that the specializations
(1.r+1)
and
(2.r+1)
allow us to compute the specializations
(1.r)
and
(2.r)
. Since the specializations
(1.r)
are a subset of the specializations
(1.r+1)
we only need to focus on computing
(2.r)

P(x,q_{k}x,\ldots,q_{k}^{r-1}x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})

According to Lemma
7
with
c_{i}=q_{k}^{i-1}
for
1\leq i\leq r
we have

P(x,q_{k}x,\dots,q_{k}^{r-1}x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})\\
=\frac{G(x,x_{r+1},\dots,x_{n})}{\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{j=r+1}^{n}(q_{k}^{i-1}x-x_{j})^{2}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq r}(q_{k}^{i-1}-q_{k}^{j-1})^{2}\prod_{r+1\leq i<j\leq n}(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}

where
G
is a polynomial of degree
2r(n-r)
in
x
.
According to the wheel conditions (
35
), it has the following factorization:

G(x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})=\prod_{i=0}^{r-2}\prod_{j=r+1}^{n}(q_{k^{\prime}}x_{j}-q_{k}^{i}x)(q_{k^{\prime\prime}}x_{j}-q_{k}^{i}x)H(x,x_{r},\ldots,x_{n})

with the degree of
H
in
x
being
2r(n-r)-2(r-1)(n-r)=2(n-r)
.
To determine
H
, we therefore need to know its values at
2(n-r)+1
distinct
specializations of
x
. The value of
H
at
x=0
gives one specialization and can be computed from the knowledge of

\lim_{x_{r}\to 0}\cdots\lim_{x_{1}\to 0}P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})

The specialization (2), using the fact that
P
is a symmetric polynomial, is equivalent to computing
H
at
x=q_{k}^{-r}x_{j}
and
x=q_{k}x_{j}
,
j=r+1,\ldots,n
.
This is the required number of specializations, which means
G
is determined and with it
P(x,q_{k}x,\ldots,q_{k}^{r-1}x,x_{r+1},\ldots,x_{n})
.
∎

Let us apply this result to the computation of the basis elements
\epsilon_{\lambda}(q_{k})
with
\ell(\lambda)=m
and
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{m}=n
. For convenience we only consider the case
k=3
and denote by

\epsilon_{\lambda}=\epsilon_{\lambda}(q_{3})=\epsilon_{\lambda}(t)

We apply Proposition
9
with
r=n
and
k=3
to the problem of computing
\epsilon_{\lambda}
. This means that in order to determine
\epsilon_{\lambda}
we need to know the values of

(62)

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{i}\to 0}\cdots\lim_{x_{1}\to 0}\epsilon_{\lambda}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}),\qquad i=1,\dots,n-1

(63)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(x,tx,\ldots,t^{n-1}x)

One can compute the limits in (
62
) using the following lemma.

Lemma 8
.

The elements
\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}}
, with
\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{m}=n
, satisfy the recurrence relation

(64)

\displaystyle\lim_{x_{n}\rightarrow 0}\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1},x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}-1,\dots,\lambda_{m}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})

Proof.

We start by writing
\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}}
explicitly as a shuffle product of
\epsilon_{\lambda_{l}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}}}\prod_{l=1}^{m}\epsilon_{\lambda_{l}}(x_{w(1+\sum_{i=1}^{l-1}\lambda_{i})},\dots,x_{w(\sum_{i=1}^{l}\lambda_{i})})

(65)

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq a<b\leq m}\prod_{i\in I_{a},j\in I_{b}}\omega(x_{w(j)},x_{w(i)})

where we defined the subsets
I_{a}
by

(66)

I_{a}:=\left\{\sum_{s=1}^{a-1}\lambda_{s}+1,\dots,\sum_{s=1}^{a}\lambda_{s}\right\}

From definitions (
43
) and (
38
) we have two obvious identities

\displaystyle\epsilon_{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1},0)=\epsilon_{k-1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k-1})\qquad\omega(x,0)=\omega(0,x)=1

Fix
r\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and consider the summands of (
3.4
) with
w
such that
n\in w(I_{r})
. The factor
\epsilon_{\lambda_{r}}
will be replaced by
\epsilon_{\lambda_{r}-1}
upon setting
x_{n}=0
while the factors of
\omega
containing
x_{n}
will disappear. The summation over
w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
(with
n\in w(I_{r})
) reduces to the summation over
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}/\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}-1,\dots,\lambda_{m}}
which is equal to the shuffle product of
\epsilon_{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\epsilon_{\lambda_{r}-1},\dots,\epsilon_{\lambda_{m}}
similarly to (
3.4
). Treating in this way every summand with
n\in w(I_{r})
for
r\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
gives rise to the formula (
64
).
∎

It is clear that computing (
62
) can be done inductively using (
64
).
The specializations in (
63
) are not difficult to compute and in fact follow from more general specializations considered in
[
FHH+09
]
. We have

(67)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\lambda}(x,tx,\ldots,t^{n-1}x)=0,\qquad\lambda\neq(1,1,\dots,1)

(68)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{(1,1,\dots,1)}(x,tx,\ldots,t^{n-1}x)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{(qt^{i}-t^{j})(t^{i+1}-qt^{j})(t^{i}-t^{j+1})}{qt(t^{i}-t^{j})^{3}}

Let us explain (
68
). We use the explicit expression (
3.4
) with
\lambda_{i}=1
in which case all
\epsilon_{\lambda_{i}}=1
and all sets
I_{a}
have a single element. Due to the vanishing of
\omega(tx,x)=0
only one term in the summation is non-vanishing. This term must have
x
’s with fully ordered indices as follows

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\omega(x_{i},x_{j})|_{x_{k}=t^{k-1}x}

which is equal to the product on the right hand side of (
68
).

3.5.
The shuffle element
\kappa_{n}

The results of this section are gathered in the following proposition:

Proposition 10
.

Let
\kappa_{0}=1\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Each of the following formulas uniquely determines the same sequence of shuffle algebra elements
\kappa_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
:

(69)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\prime}(\kappa_{n})=\sigma_{q}(e_{n})

(70)

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\kappa_{j}*\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})\qquad k=1,2,3

(71)

\displaystyle\left(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\right)\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{n-1}*x_{1}

where in (
70
) any one choice
k\in\{1,2,3\}
suffices.

Proof.

It is clear that each of these equations uniquely determines the element
\kappa_{n}
, we only need to show their consistency. We can take (
71
) together with
\kappa_{0}=1
as the definition of
\kappa_{n}
and prove that it implies (
70
) and (
69
).

The derivation of (
70
) is recursive. The case
n=0
of (
70
) obviously holds. Using (
71
) we will derive (
70
) for
\kappa_{n}
assuming that (
70
) holds for
n-1
. We act with
*\,x_{1}
on both sides of (
70
) where
n
is replaced by
n-1

(72)

\displaystyle\kappa_{n-1}*x_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n+1}(q_{k}-1)^{n-1-j}\kappa_{j}*\epsilon_{n-j-1}(q_{k})*x_{1}

On the left hand side we apply (
71
) and on the right hand side (
59
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\kappa_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\kappa_{j}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-j}x_{i}\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j-1}\kappa_{j}*x_{1}*\epsilon_{n-j-1}(q_{k})

On the right hand side we apply (
71
) to
\kappa_{j}*x_{1}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\kappa_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\kappa_{j}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-j}x_{i}\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j+1}x_{i}\kappa_{j+1}\right)*\epsilon_{n-j-1}(q_{k})

In the last summation we can shift the index
j

(73)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\kappa_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\kappa_{j}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-j}x_{i}\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}x_{i}\kappa_{j}\right)*\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})

From the definition of the shuffle product (
36
) it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}*\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-j}x_{i}\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})\right)+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{j}x_{i}\kappa_{j}\right)*\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\right)\kappa_{j}*\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})

Using this identity in (
73
) for every pair of summands with
j=1,\dots,n-1
leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\kappa_{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n}\kappa_{0}*\epsilon_{n}(q_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}q_{k}^{-n}\kappa_{n}*\epsilon_{0}(q_{k})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}q_{k}^{-n}(q_{k}-1)^{n-j}\kappa_{j}*\epsilon_{n-j}(q_{k})

After removing the common factor
\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}
and putting all terms together on the right hand side we obtain (
70
) for
\kappa_{n}
.

Next we show that (
69
) holds. For that we will use (
70
). It is sufficient to consider
k=1
in (
70
). We apply the map
\Upsilon^{\prime}
to this equation

(74)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\prime}(\kappa_{n})=\sum_{j=0}^{n}q^{n}(q^{-1}-1)^{n-j}\Upsilon^{\prime}(\kappa_{j})\Upsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon_{n-j}(q^{-1}))

From (
56
) we have
\Upsilon^{\prime}(\epsilon_{n-j}(q^{-1}))=e_{n-j}
. Let us denote by
e^{\prime}_{n}=\Upsilon^{\prime}(\kappa_{n})
, then (
74
) becomes

(75)

\displaystyle e^{\prime}_{n}=\sum_{j=0}^{n}q^{j}(1-q)^{n-j}e^{\prime}_{j}e_{n-j}

We need to show that
e^{\prime}_{n}=\sigma_{q}(e_{n})
. Note that, together with the initial condition, (
75
) uniquely determines
e^{\prime}_{n}
. Let us solve (
75
) using generating functions, set

(76)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(z):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{\prime}_{n}z^{n}\qquad E(z):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e_{n}z^{n}=\exp\left(-\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}p_{j}z^{j}\right)

Multiply both sides of (
75
) by
z^{n}
and sum over
n\geq 0

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(z)=E^{\prime}(qz)E((1-q)z)

By iterating this equation we find

\displaystyle E^{\prime}(z)=

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}E((1-q)q^{k}z)=\exp\left(-\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j}(1-q)^{j}}{j}p_{j}q^{jk}z^{j}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j}(1-q)^{j}}{j(1-q^{j})}p_{j}z^{j}\right)=\exp\left(-\sum_{j>0}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\sigma_{q}(p_{j})z^{j}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sigma_{q}(e_{n})z^{n}

Comparing the last result with (
76
) shows that
e^{\prime}_{n}=\sigma_{q}(e_{n})
.
∎

4.
Matching the partition functions with shuffle elements

We now investigate the partition functions associated to the elements

A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}
(where
A_{n}
was defined in (
12
)) that were studied in the previous section on the shuffle algebra side.

Proposition 11
.

Given
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
with
n=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}
, one has the limit

(77)

\lim_{x_{n}\to 0}x_{n}^{n-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})=(-1)^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}x_{i}\,\sum_{i=1}^{m}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{i}-1}*A_{\lambda_{m}})

Note that by commutativity,

\lambda
can be chosen to be a partition.

Proof.

We start from the definition of
A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}
(cf (
13
)):

A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-1)^{|v|}t^{-(|v|+|w|)}T_{w}T_{v}T_{w^{-1}}

By definition of the groups involved, one has
T_{w}T_{v}=T_{wv}
(and similarly
T_{v}T_{w^{-1}}=T_{vw^{-1}}
, but we choose to use the first identity).

Plugging this into (
27
), we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-1)^{|v|}t^{-(|v|+|w|)}\left<Z|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}(T_{w}T_{v}T_{w^{-1}}\otimes S_{n})\right>

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-1)^{|v|}t^{-(|v|+|w|)}\left<T_{w^{-1}}Z|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}T_{wv}(1\otimes S_{n})\right>

Using (
30
), we can rewrite this as

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-1)^{|v|}t^{-(|v|+|w|)}\left\langle 1\right|T_{w^{-1}}Z|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}T_{wv}\left|1\right\rangle

(78)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-t)^{-|v|}\left\langle w^{-1}\right|Z|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}\left|v^{-1}w^{-1}\right\rangle

This has the following interpretation:

\alpha_{n}^{-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})
is the sum over
v
and
w
of partition functions of lattice paths,
where the paths are labelled
w^{-1}(1),\ldots,w^{-1}(n)
on the left side and

v^{-1}(w^{-1}(1)),\ldots,v^{-1}(w^{-1}(n))
on the top side (with an extra weight
(-t)^{-|v|}
).

We can now take the limit
x_{n}\to 0
. In this paragraph we denote by
a=w^{-1}
,
b=v^{-1}w^{-1}
,
which are two arbitrary permutations, and consider
\left\langle a\right|Z|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}\left|b\right\rangle
as
x_{n}\to 0
.
We are only interested for the purposes of deriving (
77
) in the terms of order
x_{n}^{-n+1}
;
from Lemma
3
we know that this is the minimum order in
x_{n}
and in fact, as in the proof of the lemma,
we know that the only dependence on
x_{n}
comes from the last row and column of the diagram of
Z
. Let us examine
these in more detail with our boundary conditions
\left\langle a\right|
and
\left|b\right\rangle
.
We look at the destiny of the path
b(n)
which ends at the top right: to maximize the degree in
x_{n}^{-1}
, we must
pick up a
x_{n}^{-1}
at each vertex
(i,n)
,
i=1,\ldots,n-1
; this means, according to (
3
) that the first
type of vertex (a path making a bend when the right edge is either empty or has a higher labelled path) must be excluded.
Since all right edges are empty in the last column, one can prove inductively that the paths must go straight through
every vertex
(i,n)
,
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, incurring a weight of
-q^{-1}x_{i}x_{n}^{-1}
.
At vertex
(n,n)
, because both bottom and right edges are empty, we must have the first type of vertex in (
3
),
incurring a
1-t
. From there, the path can only go straight left to its destination with label
a(n)
, incurring
a weight
t^{n-1}
and imposing
a(n)=b(n)
. Combining all this, we find

(79)

\left\langle a\right|Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n})\left|b\right\rangle=\delta_{a(n),b(n)}(1-t)t^{n-1}x_{n}^{-n+1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(-q^{-1}x_{i})\\
\times\left\langle a(1),\ldots,a(n-1)\right|Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n-1})\left|b(1),\ldots,b(n-1)\right\rangle+O(x_{n}^{-n+2})\qquad a,b\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(1)}

We apply this formula to
f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})
.
We fix
w\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
and denote by
k=w^{-1}(n)
. Because of the form of
w
,
k
must be the last element of its block
I_{a}
,
1\leq a\leq m
(
I_{a}
was defined in (
66
)).
According to (
79
),
the summation can be restricted to
v
such that
v(k)=k
. Consider now the permutation
w^{\prime}
with
w^{\prime}(i)=w(i)
,
i=1,\ldots,k-1
,
w^{\prime}(i)=w(i+1)
,
i=k,\ldots,n-1
, and
the permutation
v^{\prime}
with
v^{\prime}(i)=i
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
and
v^{\prime}(i)=v(i+1)-1
for
i=k,\ldots,n-1
. Because
k
is last of its block,

|v^{\prime}|=|v|
and we have

\alpha_{n}^{-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})\overset{x_{n}\to 0}{\sim}(1-t)t^{n-1}x_{n}^{-n+1}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(-q^{-1}x_{i})\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{a}-1,\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}(-t)^{-|v^{\prime}|}\sum_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{a}-1,\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}\\
\left\langle w^{\prime-1}(1),\ldots,w^{\prime-1}(n-1)\right|Z(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-1},qx_{1},\ldots,qx_{n-1})\left|v^{-1}(w^{\prime-1}(1)),\ldots,v^{\prime-1}(w^{\prime-1}(n-1))\right\rangle

Comparing with (
78
) and using

\alpha_{n}^{-1}\alpha_{n-1}=(1-t)(t/q)^{n-1}
, we finally find the desired
limit (
77
).
∎

In what follows, we shall use the diagrammatic language.
We shall repeatedly use the following simple identities:

Lemma 9
.

One has for
k\leq\ell,m\geq 2
:

(80)

\scriptstyle A_{k}
\scriptstyle A_{\ell}
\scriptstyle\cdots
\scriptstyle\cdots
\scriptstyle\cdots
\scriptstyle\cdots
\scriptstyle\cdots

This immediately implies

Lemma 10
.

For any
c\in{\mathrm{Z}}
,
f(c)
satisfies the wheel conditions

f(c)(x,tx,qx,x_{4},\ldots,x_{n})=f(c)(tqx,tx,qx,x_{4},\ldots,x_{n})=0

Proof.

f(c)
being symmetric in its arguments according to Proposition
4
,
we can pick any three variables and specialize them.
Let us then consider the diagram above and set
x_{n-2}=x
,
x_{n-1}=tx
,
x_{n}=qx
.
Zooming in on the bottom right corner, we have, using
\check{R}(t^{-1})=(1-t)A_{2}
,

Switching rows and columns results similarly in the second wheel condition.
∎

We now go back to
A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}
.
Starting from the expression

f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})=\alpha_{n}\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}}t^{-|w|}\left<(T_{w}(A_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{\lambda_{m}})T_{w^{-1}}\otimes S_{n})Z\right>|_{y_{i}=qx_{i},\ i=1,\ldots,n}

and using the cyclicity of
\left<\bullet\right>
, we can describe the expression inside it
as the diagram: (compare with (
83
))

with
\tilde{X}_{w}=\left<X_{w}\right>A_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{\lambda_{m}}\otimes S_{n}
. We shall not use
\tilde{X}_{w}
in what follows.

Proposition 12
.

•

For
m<n
, one has the vanishing condition:

(85)

f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})(x,tx,\ldots,t^{m}x,x_{m+2},\ldots,x_{n})=0

•

The partition function
f(A_{n})
has the fully factorized form

(86)

f(A_{n})=t^{-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}(1-tx_{i}/x_{j})

•

One has the specialization

(87)

f(A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1})(1,t,\ldots,t^{n-1})=[n]_{t^{-1}}!\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(q-t^{j-i})(q-t^{i-j+1})

Proof.

We first consider
m=1
, and investigate the effect of setting
x_{1}=tx
,
x_{2}=x
in
X_{w}
, where here
w=1
.
Noting that
\check{R}_{1}(t)=(1-t)(1+T_{1})=(1-t)S_{2}
, and using (
81
), we can insert a crossing at the right of the top two horizontal lines and then
apply the Yang–Baxter equation repeatedly to move the crossing to the left,
resulting in zero according to (
82
):

\displaystyle X_{1}|_{x_{1}=tx,\ x_{2}=x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+t}\ \hbox to199.57pt{\vbox to148.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.58113pt\lower 11.18114pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

We conclude that
x_{1}-tx_{2}
divides
f(A_{n})
, and by symmetry,

f(A_{n})
is a multiple of
\prod_{i\neq j}(1-tx_{i}/x_{j})
. The latter has degree range
[-(n-1),(n-1)]
in each
x_{i}
, which is the maximum allowed according to (
2
), so only a constant in
{\mathbb{F}}
remains to be determined. The latter is determined inductively by using Proposition
11
.

We proceed identically for
1<m<n
. We set
x_{1}=t^{m}x
, …,
x_{m}=tx
,
x_{m+1}=x
.
We note that the “
R
-matrix associated to the longest element
w_{0}
of
\mathcal{S}_{m+1}
”, i.e., the product of
R
-matrices whose diagram reproduces (any) wiring diagram of
w_{0}
, with spectral parameters set to
t^{m},\ldots,1
, is nothing but

(1-t)^{\frac{m(m+1)}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}[i]_{t}!\,S_{m+1}
(see e.g.
[
Las06
, Lem. 3]
or
[
IO09
, 3.1]
), and move it across to the left using the Yang–Baxter equation:

X_{w}|_{x_{1}=t^{m}x,\ldots,x_{m+1}=x}=\frac{1}{[m+1]_{t}!}\ \hbox to216.64pt{\vbox to172.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.72458pt\lower 11.18114pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The pigeonhole principle tells us there will be two numbers between
1
and
m+1
whose preimages under
w

lie in the same block (i.e., are associated to the same part of
\lambda
). Since
w\in\mathcal{S}^{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
, all numbers whose preimages are in the same block
are consecutive, and so we can apply (
82
) to conclude that the result is zero. By summing over
w
and using symmetry in the exchange of variables, we obtain the result (
85
) as stated in the Proposition.

The only case left is
m=n
, i.e.,
A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1}
. We set
x_{1}=t^{n-1}x,\ldots,x_{n-1}=tx,x_{n}=x
but this time the result
does not vanish. However, noting once again that
S_{n}
is up to normalization
the product of
R
-matrices associated to the longest permutation
w_{0}
of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
we can move it to the left and absorb any crossing from
T_{w}
:

X_{w}|_{x_{1}=t^{n-1}x,\ldots,x_{n}=x}=\hbox to195.21pt{\vbox to174.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.94344pt\lower 9.65625pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

where in the last line we find it convenient to reintroduce the longest element
w_{0}
, before moving back
S_{n}
to the right.
Performing the summation over
w
(one could instead absorb the top crossings as well, but the result would be the same), we find

We then impose
y_{i}=qx_{i}
and note that up to normalization, the
S_{n}
at the top is nothing but the
R
-matrix
associated to the longest element of
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{(2)}
; we can pull it to the bottom:

\sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}t^{-|w|}X_{w}|_{y_{i}=qx_{i}}=t^{-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}}\ \hbox to206.86pt{\vbox to162.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.94344pt\lower-11.58113pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

This is essentially the same situation that was considered at the end of §
2
. First we expand each
R
-matrix in the upper-left triangle as

\check{R}_{i}(u)=u(1-t)+t(1-u)T_{i}^{-1}
and note that the only term that has a nonzero identity coefficient is

f(A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1})=\alpha_{n}[n]_{t}!(1-t)^{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
i+j<n+1\end{subarray}}(1-q^{-1}t^{n-i-j-1})\left<\hbox to206.86pt{\vbox to139.82pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.94344pt\lower 9.93816pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
