Secondly, we absorb all the crossings of the lower-right triangle into
S_{n}
,
resulting in a weight of
\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
i+j>n+1\end{subarray}}(1-q^{-1}t^{n-i-j+2})
. Recombining and simplifying, we obtain (
87
).
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
1
.
First, combining Lemma
3
, Proposition
4
and Lemma
10
,
we find that
f(c)
, from its definition (
27
), is

\alpha_{n}
times an element of
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
.
\alpha_{n}
contains a factor of
(1-t)^{-n}
, but it is compensated by the fact that any lattice path entering from the left and exiting from the top must have a bend, resulting in a factor of
(1-t)^{n}
according to (
3
) in the expression (
4
) of
f(c)
. Therefore,
f
is a map from
{\mathrm{Z}}({\mathcal{H}}_{n})
to
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
.

Next, we identify
f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})
.
We use the characterization of Proposition
9
, so that

\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}
is entirely determined by Lemma
8
and (
68
).
On the other hand, it is easy to check that Proposition
12
implies the exact same relations for
V_{n}^{-1}f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})
(where
V_{n}
is defined in (
42
), and implements the isomorphism
V_{n}\times
from
\mathcal{A}_{n}
to
{\mathrm{A}}_{n}
). Therefore,

f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})=V_{n}\,\epsilon_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m}}

We finally compare
\Upsilon(f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}))
and
\Phi(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})
.

According to the ring map property of
\Upsilon
and (
56
) applied to
\epsilon_{r}=\epsilon_{r}^{(3)}
,

\displaystyle\Upsilon(f(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}}))

\displaystyle=\Upsilon(V_{n}\,\epsilon^{(3)}_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*\epsilon^{(3)}_{\lambda_{m}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Upsilon(V_{\lambda_{i}}\,\epsilon^{(3)}_{\lambda_{i}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{q^{-1}}\sigma_{t}^{-1}(e_{\lambda_{i}})

On the other hand, from (
17
) and the ring map property of
\Phi
,

\Phi(A_{\lambda_{1}}*\cdots*A_{\lambda_{m}})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}e_{\lambda_{i}}

We have therefore checked the statement of Theorem
1
on a basis of
{\mathrm{Z}}
.

Corollary 1
.

We have the following identity

(88)

f(1_{n})=V_{n}\kappa_{n}

Proof.

According to (
69
) and Proposition
8
,
\Upsilon(V_{n}\kappa_{n})=\Upsilon^{\prime}(\kappa_{n})=\sigma_{q}(e_{n})
. On the other hand,
according to Proposition
3
,
\Phi(1_{n})=\tilde{h}_{n}=\sigma_{t}(h_{n})=\sigma_{t}\sigma_{\infty}(e_{n})
.
Applying Theorem
1
, we conclude that (
88
) holds.
∎

Using the definition of the shuffle product (
36
) we can write

(89)

\left(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\right)\kappa_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\kappa_{n-1}(x_{1},\ldots,\hat{x_{i}},\ldots,x_{n})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(x_{i}-q_{1}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{2}x_{j})(x_{i}-q_{3}x_{j})}{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{3}}

The recurrence relation for
f(1_{n})
stated in (
5
) follows from (
88
), (
89
) and (
42
) thus proving Theorem
2
.

5.
Application to the commuting scheme

5.1.
Proof of Theorem
3

In this section, all schemes are over
{\mathbb{C}}
, as in the introduction.

Proposition 13
.

Assuming Conjecture
1
,

K_{n}
satisfies the following recurrence relation:

(90)

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})K_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}K_{n-1}[\hat{x_{i}}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(1-q_{1}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{2}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{1}q_{2}x_{i}/x_{j})}{1-x_{j}/x_{i}}

where
K_{n-1}[\hat{x_{i}}]=K_{n-1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})
.

Proof.

For the purposes of this proof, we need more geometry than in the rest of the paper.
We think of the multi-graded Hilbert series as the pushforward to a point in localized equivariant
K
-theory. Equivalently, the
K
-polynomial of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
is viewed as (the pushforward of) the
K_{T}
-class
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n}}]
in the ambient space
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
. Note that
K_{T}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n})\cong K_{T}(pt)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}[q_{1}^{\pm},q_{2}^{\pm},x_{1}^{\pm},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm}]
where the variables
q_{1},q_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are the
inverses
of the equivariant parameters attached to the torus
T
.

Consider

\mathfrak{F}_{n}=\left\{(v,A,B)\in{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}:\ [A,B]=0\text{ and $v$ eigenvector of $A$ and $B$}\right\}

There is a natural projective morphism

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{n}

\displaystyle\overset{p}{\to}\mathfrak{C}_{n}

\displaystyle(v,A,B)

\displaystyle\mapsto(A,B)

which is generically
n
-to-
1
(choice of one eigenvector among
n
).
There is also the affine morphism
f:\mathfrak{F}_{n}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
. Since all fibers are isomorphic by
GL_{n}
-action, the dimension of a fiber is
\dim\mathfrak{F}_{n}-\dim{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}=n^{2}+1
.

The computation is in two parts:

•

Computing
\pi_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]
by equivariant localization (which relates it to
\pi_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}]
using

(v,A,B)\mapsto(A|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/v},B|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}/v})
).

•

Comparing
p_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]
and
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n}}]
.

5.1.1.
Equivariant localization

Torus fixed points in
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
are basis vectors
e_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Consider such a fixed point, which up to
\mathcal{S}_{n}
action, we can choose to be
v=e_{n}
. The fiber is

f^{-1}(v)=\{v\}\times X,\qquad X=\left\{\left(A=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&0\\
\alpha&a\end{pmatrix},B=\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime}&0\\
\beta&b\end{pmatrix}\right):\ AB=BA\right\}

More explicitly, the equations are

(91)

\displaystyle A^{\prime}B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=B^{\prime}A^{\prime}

(92)

\displaystyle a\beta+\alpha B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=b\alpha+\beta A^{\prime}

In particular note that
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}
. There are
2n
extra variables
\alpha,\beta,a,b
and
n-1
extra equations, and

\dim\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}+2n-(n-1)=n^{2}+1=\dim f^{-1}(v)
, so assuming Conjecture
1
(Cohen–Macaulay property for
\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}
, which implies it for
\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}\times\mathbb{A}^{2n+2}
), the unmixedness theorem asserts that the extra equations (
92
) form a regular sequence.

We then conclude by standard exact sequence arguments that

(93)

[\mathcal{O}_{X}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}]\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}(1-q_{1}x_{j}/x_{n})(1-q_{2}x_{j}/x_{n})(1-q_{1}q_{2}x_{n}/x_{j})

where
[\mathcal{O}_{X}]
is the
K_{T}
class of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
in
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
,
whereas
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}]
is the
K_{T}
class of
\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}
in
\mathfrak{gl}_{n-1}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n-1}
(where the
T
-action on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n-1}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n-1}
has a one-dimensional kernel leading to the obvious inclusion

K_{T/{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}}\subset K_{T}
, i.e., as a polynomial
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}]=K_{n-1}
is independent of
x_{n}
).
The factors
1-q_{1}x_{j}/x_{n}
(resp.
1-q_{2}x_{j}/x_{n}
) come from the zero column of
A
(resp.
B
) whereas the factors
1-q_{1}q_{2}x_{n}/x_{j}
come from the equations (
92
).

We have thus managed to compute
[f^{-1}(v)]=[v][\mathcal{O}_{X}]
.

The weights at the tangent space of
e_{i}
are
x_{i}/x_{j}
,
j\neq i
(recalling that the
x_{i}
are the inverses of the equivariant parameters). Pulling back the relation

1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{[e_{i}]}{\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}(1-x_{j}/x_{i})}

in localized equivariant
K
-theory of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
and multiplying by
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]
, we obtain

[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}[e_{i}][\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}](\hat{x}_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(1-q_{2}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{1}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{1}q_{2}x_{i}/x_{j})}{1-x_{j}/x_{i}}

Finally, pushing forward to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
leads to:

(94)

p_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\,[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}](\hat{x}_{i})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n}\frac{(1-q_{2}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{1}x_{j}/x_{i})(1-q_{1}q_{2}x_{i}/x_{j})}{1-x_{j}/x_{i}}

5.1.2.
Pushforward

Now let us try to compute directly the pushforward of
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]
under
p:\mathfrak{F}_{n}\to\mathfrak{C}_{n}
.
Write
\mathfrak{C}_{n}=\operatorname{Spec}R
and
\mathfrak{F}_{n}=\operatorname{Proj}S

where
S_{0}=R
.
Since
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
is affine, the pushforward is simply given in terms of the sheaf cohomology groups of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)
(viewed as modules over
R
).
We claim the following

H^{i}(\mathfrak{F}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1))\cong\begin{cases}S_{1}&i=0\\
0&i>0\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us first write the equations of
\mathfrak{F}_{n}
explicitly:

(95)

AB=BA\qquad\text{minors of size $2$ of $(v,Av)$ and $(v,Bv)$}=0

Now define the patches
U_{i}=\{v_{i}\neq 0\}
.
Let us consider
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
inside
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
. Denote by
z=v_{1}/v_{2}
,
r_{i}=v_{i}/v_{2}
,
i>2
and collectively
r=(r_{i})
. A section on
U_{1}
is given by a polynomial
P(z,r,A,B)
modulo the relations (
95
) rewritten in terms of
z,r,A,B
. Let us call these equations
q_{i}(z)
where dependence on
r,A,B
is suppressed. Note that the
q_{i}(z)
are of degree less or equal to
2
in
z
.
Similarly a section on
U_{2}
is given by a polynomial
Q(z^{-1},rz^{-1},A,B)
modulo the same equations expressed in terms of
z^{-1},rz^{-1},A,B
. The
\mathcal{O}(1)
matching condition on
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
is

(96)

P(z)-zQ(z^{-1})=\sum_{i}c_{i}(z)q_{i}(z)

where the
c_{i}(z)
are Laurent polynomials in
z
.
Now assume that
P
is of degree greater than
1
in
z
. Then its top coefficient is equal to the top coefficient of
Q:=\sum_{i}c^{pol}_{i}(z)q_{i}(z)
where
c^{pol}_{i}(z)
is the part of
c_{i}(z)
with nonnegative powers of
z
. By subtracting
Q
from
P
, we can reduce the degree of
P
until it is at most
1
.

This way, one can show that the restriction of a global section to
U_{i}
is of degree in
v_{i}/v_{j}
at most
1
for all
i\neq j
; this immediately implies that the
v_{i}
generate the global sections of
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Linear independence is obvious (it can be tested at the generic point).

(
96
) also implies that the map
(P,Q)\mapsto P(z)-zQ(z^{-1})
is surjective, so that
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1))=0
. Vanishing of higher cohomology follows similarly.
∎

Since
H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1))\cong S_{1}
is a free
R
-module with basis
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
and higher sheaf cohomology vanishes, we conclude that

(97)

p_{*}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{F}_{n}}(1)]=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{C}_{n}}]\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}

where
x_{j}
is the weight of the basis element
v_{j}
.

Combining (
94
) and (
97
) leads to the recurrence relation (
90
).
∎

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
3

by relating the shuffle element
\kappa_{n}
and the
K
-polynomial
K_{n}
.
K_{n}
is uniquely determined by (
90
) and the initial condition
K_{0}=1
. Similarly
\kappa_{n}
can be computed recursively with (
89
). By comparing (
89
) with (
90
) (and checking the trivial initial condition
\kappa_{0}=K_{0}=1
) we conclude that

(98)

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\frac{t^{n(n-1)/2}}{V_{n}(x)}K_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

Combining (
88
) with (
98
) gives us the statement of Theorem
3
.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
4

Theorem
4
involves computing the multidegree of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
, which means one needs to work in equivariant cohomology.
The situation is much simpler than in equivariant
K
-theory (which was needed for Theorem
3
), and we only sketch the corresponding geometric argument. By abuse of notation, we use the same symbols

q_{1},q_{2},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
for the cohomology equivariant parameters. This is
consistent with our passage from
K
-polynomial to multidegree, which was defined in the introduction as the substitution
q_{i}\mapsto 1-q_{i}
and
x_{i}\mapsto 1-x_{i}
and then expanding at leading order. We refer to this limit in what follows as the GRR (Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch) limit.

View the flag variety
\mathcal{B}_{n}
as the variety of Borel subalgebras of

\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
and consider

\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}=\{(\mathfrak{b},A,B)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}\times\mathfrak{gl}_{n}:\ [A,B]=0,\ A,B\in\mathfrak{b}\}
.
There is a projection map
p
from
\tilde{\mathfrak{F}}_{n}
to
\mathfrak{C}_{n}

which is generically
n!
-to-
1
(generic matrices are diagonalizable);
one then obtains by equivariant localization techniques (similar to §
5.1.1
) the formula

\displaystyle D_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}(q_{1}+x_{i}-x_{j})(q_{2}+x_{i}-x_{j})\ \sum_{w\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}w\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{q_{1}+q_{2}+x_{i}-x_{j}}{(x_{j}-x_{i})(q_{1}+x_{i}-x_{j})(q_{2}+x_{i}-x_{j})}\right)

(99)

\displaystyle=\text{Sym}\left(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{(q_{1}+q_{2}+x_{i}-x_{j})(q_{1}-x_{i}+x_{j})(q_{2}-x_{i}+x_{j})}{x_{j}-x_{i}}\right)

where the symmetric group acts by permuting variables, and
Sym
denotes symmetrization w.r.t. to it.

One can think of this expression as the GRR limit of the shuffle element

\frac{1}{n!}f(A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1})
(studied in §
4
).
2
2

2

It should be noted
that the element
f(A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1})
itself has a geometric interpretation

[
Gin11
]
as the Hilbert series of the coordinate ring of the normalization of the isospectral commuting variety
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathrm{norm}}
.
What we are finding here is that to go from
K
-theory to cohomology, instead of using the structure sheaf of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
one can use the combinatorially simpler structure sheaf of
\mathfrak{X}_{\mathrm{norm}}
.

At
t=1
, from (
1
), one has the identity in the center of the symmetric group algebras

A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1}=n!\,1_{n}
, so that in the GRR limit,
\frac{1}{n!}f(A_{1}*\cdots*A_{1})
and
f(1_{n})
coincide. As was already remarked in §
1.4
,
expanding the weights (
8
), which correspond to
f(1_{n})
, in the GRR limit, result in the weights (
9
).
This leads to the formula of Theorem
4
.

A few comments are in order. In
[
DFZJ06
,
KZJ07
]
another expression for the multidegree of the commuting variety was obtained, using apparently unrelated methods.
There is actually a connection: consider Proposition
6

and take the GRR limit.
One recovers one of the exchange relations of the work above (cf
[
DFZJ06
, Eq. (3.10)]
or
[
KZJ07
, Prop. 6]
). The expression for the multidegree of
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
there is essentially a repeated application of these identities; it is not obvious if it is more or less useful than our (new) formula (
99
).
(The extension of these ideas to
K
-theory will be investigated
elsewhere
[
KZJ
]
.)

The expression of Theorem
4
, in contrast, has many desirable properties; for instance, it is division- and subtraction-free. Regarding positivity, let us only consider the bidegree with respect to scaling of the two matrices
A
and
B
, i.e., set the
x_{i}
to zero. On the one hand, (
99
) specializes to the bidegree being the constant term of the Laurent polynomial

\frac{1}{n!}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{(u_{i}-u_{j})(q_{1}-u_{i}+u_{j})(q_{2}-u_{i}+u_{j})(q_{1}+q_{2}+u_{i}-u_{j})}{u_{i}u_{j}}
, which clearly involves subtractions.
On the other hand, the expression in (
10
) is a polynomial in
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
with manifestly positive coefficients. If we further restrict to the ordinary degree, i.e., set
q_{1}=q_{2}=1
, then we have the
simple formula

(100)

\deg\mathfrak{C}_{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{lattice paths }P\\
\text{on the }n\times n\text{ grid}\\
\text{with identity connectivity}\end{subarray}}2^{\#\{(i,j):\text{ bends}\}-n}

(
100
) is also extremely effective computationally; here is
the degree of
\mathfrak{C}_{12}
(computed in a few seconds on a laptop):

\deg\mathfrak{C}_{12}=1862632561783036151478238040096092649

(note that this is beyond the currently known entries of

OEIS A029729
).

5.3.
The
\mathcal{S}_{3}
symmetry

As an obvious corollary of (
71
) of Proposition
10
, one has:

Corollary 2
.

\kappa_{n}
possesses the
\mathcal{S}_{3}
symmetry of permutations of
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
.

In view of (
88
), the same holds for
f(1_{n})
; this symmetry is however not obvious at the level of the partition function.

In a similar vein, assuming Conjecture
1
, this also implies
the symmetry property

K_{n}(q_{1},q_{2})=K_{n}(q_{2},q_{1})=(q_{1}q_{2}^{2})^{n(n-1)/2}K_{n}(q_{1},(q_{1}q_{2})^{-1})

Example 5
.

Here are the coefficients of
K_{3}|_{x_{i}=1}
:
1
-8
1
1
1
29
-16
1
1
-16
-27
29
-8
1
1
29
-16
1
-8
1
1
1
\scriptstyle q_{1}
\scriptstyle q_{2}

It is not a priori obvious why the
K
-polynomial of the commuting scheme
\mathfrak{C}_{n}
should possess the second symmetry property. Here we propose an ‘‘explanation’’:

3
3

3

This is to be compared with the following two toroidal algebra representations: the one coming from the Hilbert scheme of points in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, which does not have manifest
\mathcal{S}_{3}
symmetry, and the one coming from the Hilbert scheme of points in
{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
, which does.

Conjecture 2
.

Consider the triple commuting scheme

\hat{\mathfrak{C}}_{n}:=\{(A,B,C)\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{3}:\ [A,B]=[A,C]=[B,C]=0\}

and its grading w.r.t. scaling
(A,B,C)\mapsto(q_{1}A,q_{2}B,q_{3}C)
and conjugating by diagonal matrices.
Then its
K
-polynomial
\hat{K}_{n}
satisfies

\hat{K}_{n}(q_{1},q_{2},(q_{1}q_{2})^{-1})=K_{n}(q_{1},q_{2})K_{n}(q_{1}^{-1},q_{2}^{-1})
